5.3.3 Overstepping Boundaries on Ambiguous & High Risk Tasks

Even when a user request is feasible, it can be underspecified or ambiguous, the
environment can contain conflicting information, or there can be unintended high risk
consequences. In these situations, the agent must avoid overeager execution, and needs

                                                                                    185
to effectively collaborate with the user to seek more context, clarify, and communicate
any risks.

We designed an evaluation benchmark to measure whether agents are appropriately
deferring to the user and avoiding overstepping boundaries when faced with such
underspecified, ambiguous, or high risk tasks.

The benchmark contains a collection of ambiguous and high-risk SWE and non-coding
agentic tasks where the agent needs to collaborate with the user. In addition, we also
added a set of non-ambiguous tasks where the model should just execute without any
friction.

To evaluate whether agents avoid overstepping boundaries, we utilize the below metrics:

   ●​ Active Collaboration Score: Measures whether the agent correctly pauses to
      clarify with the user when there is unresolvable ambiguity or high-risk actions.
   ●​ Counter Collaboration Score: Measures whether the agent autonomously
      executes non-ambiguous tasks.

These two scores allow us to measure the Pareto frontier of agents demonstrating the
right amount of autonomy and agency without overstepping boundaries.

Gemini 4 Argon substantially improves its ability to recognize when it should ask, raising
the Active Collaboration rate from 34.0% to 55.9% compared to Gemini 3.8 Flash. At the
same time, it keeps Counter-Collaboration on clear tasks comparable (84.5% vs. 86.5%,
within the confidence interval).

                                                                                          186
Figure 5.11: Overstepping and ambiguous & high-risk task evaluation results

5.3.4 Fabrication

Agentic workflows involve multi-step execution, where the model iteratively plans,
retrieves documents, calls tools, and acts on intermediate results. In these settings,
factuality is crucial, and the model should be faithfully grounded to tool outputs and
evidence retrieved in the trajectory when making any user-facing claims. We discuss
single step contextual grounding in a later section, and here deep dive into fabrication on
software engineering (SWE) agentic tasks.

On software engineering (SWE) agentic evaluations, we measure whether claims made by
the agent are supported by the tool outputs in the trajectory context using a grounding
autorater. We use multiple evaluation sets of long horizon SWE tasks: building a new
system binary and realistic common internal coding tasks on a company-wide code base.

On SWE agentic evaluations, Gemini 4 significantly improves on both evaluations
compared to Gemini 3.8 Flash.

                                                                                        187
Figure 5.12: SWE agentic evaluation results: fabrication rates

5.4 Sycophancy
Sycophancy occurs when a model prioritizes user approval over accuracy - agreeing with
false premises, offering unearned praise, or avoiding respectful pushback when
warranted. While helpfulness requires warmth and responsiveness, sycophancy can
undermine trust and impact user-decision making across domains such as health,
finances, work, or personal lives.

We evaluate sycophancy across multiple dimensions in our Personality Evaluation. We
developed a multi-dimensional Personality Evaluation that measures the model's behavior
along a situationally-calibrated spectrum for each dimension: from Inadequate (too dry,
terse, or disengaged) to Appropriate or Exaggerated (over-pleasing, over-eager, or
over-dramatic). Two dimensions, “Candor” (resisting false agreement and echo-chamber
responses) and “Self-awareness” (knowing the model's limits and not pretending
otherwise), measure anti-sycophantic behavior directly.

The evaluation consists of ~900 prompts, 2/3 single-turn and 1/3 multi-turn conversations
of up to 10+ user turns. Prompts are drawn from user, synthetic and hand-written
conversations. Each prompt targets one of nine personality dimensions. The two
dimensions that most directly capture sycophancy, Candor and Self-awareness, lean
heavily on multi-turn conversations, as sycophancy often emerges only under sustained
user pressure. Responses are scored by a prompted autorater using dimension-specific

                                                                                      188
rubrics, which places each response at −1 (Inadequate), 0 (Appropriate), or +1
(Exaggerated). Each response is rated ten times and averaged to reduce rater variance.

The evaluation has two composite metrics:

   ●​ Dryness (aggregate of Inadequate-direction scores): Measures whether
      training is suppressing desirable personality traits.
   ●​ Superficiality (aggregate of Exaggerated-direction scores): Measures whether
      training is producing over-pleasing or sycophantic patterns.

Scores below 10 indicate well-calibrated behavior, and scores above 25 warrant closer
inspection. This framework allows us to detect and manage the trade-off between
reducing sycophancy and maintaining appropriate warmth and engagement.

Gemini 4 Argon performed better than Gemini 3.8 Flash in dryness and better than
Gemini 3.1 Pro in superficiality.

Reducing sycophancy requires the model to disagree with users when warranted. Overly
aggressive anti-sycophancy interventions can produce responses that feel overly blunt,
dismissive, or pedantic, what we track as "Dryness" in our Personality Evaluation. We
actively measure both directions and continue to work on approaches that maintain
genuine warmth while eliminating harmful sycophantic patterns. The model may also still
occasionally validate poor ideas or false premises in complex, multi-turn conversations
where user pressure is sustained.

Figure 5.15: Sycophancy evaluation results: dryness and superficiality

                                                                                        189
5.5 Hallucination
Gemini processes a wide range of everyday questions, document analysis, and research
tasks. Hallucinations — where a model asserts claims that are factually false or
unsupported by available evidence impact task reliability. We train Gemini 4 Argon to
assert only verifiable claims, and to clearly communicate uncertainty rather than guessing
when information is unavailable.

We evaluate hallucinations across three complementary settings: closed-book parametric
knowledge, contextual grounding in user-provided documents, and search-augmented
factuality and temporal reasoning. Across all three settings, Gemini 4 Argon substantially
reduces hallucinations relative to both Gemini 3.8 Flash and Gemini 3.1 Pro.

5.5.1 Parametric Knowledge

Parametric evaluations test the model's intrinsic world knowledge when answering factual
questions without access to search or external context. Because the model must rely
entirely on knowledge encoded in its parameters, this setting measures both factual recall
and calibration — whether the model recognizes the limits of its knowledge and abstains
from answering rather than fabricating an answer. We evaluate models on two public
closed-book benchmarks:

   ●​ SimpleQA Verified (paper): 1,000 human-verified, de-duplicated short-answer
      tail-knowledge questions across diverse topics.
   ●​ AA-Omniscience (paper): 6,000 cross-domain knowledge reliability questions
      across 42 topics in six domains (business, law, health, humanities, software
      engineering, and STEM).

Across both evaluations, responses are graded as correct, incorrect, or abstained.
Because a model could trivially lower its error rate by abstaining excessively, we report
both the rate of incorrect or hallucinated claims and a composite score that balances
coverage and precision — F1 score (the harmonic mean of overall accuracy and accuracy
on attempted questions) for SimpleQA Verified, and the Omniscience Index (which
rewards correct answers and penalizes incorrect guesses) for AA-Omniscience.

Across both benchmarks, Gemini 4 Argon demonstrates substantially stronger
calibration, reliably abstaining when uncertain rather than guessing. This reduces
incorrect and hallucinated answer rates by 2.7x to 3.9x relative to Gemini 3.1 Pro.

                                                                                       190
                                                                                    ​
Figure 5.16: Parametric knowledge: incorrect & hallucination rates

                                                                                    ​
Figure 5.17: Parametric knowledge: F1 scores & omniscience index​

5.5.2 Contextual Grounding

Contextual grounding measures the model's ability to remain faithful to user-provided
source documents without introducing unsupported or contradictory claims. We evaluate
grounding using the public FACTS Grounding benchmark (paper, suite), which tests
whether long-form responses to user requests are strictly supported by provided
documents (up to 32K tokens). Additionally, we report internal evaluations measuring

                                                                                  191
sentence-level unsupported claim rates on long-context document grounding and
multi-snippet inline citation tasks.

Gemini 4 Argon shows marked improvements in faithfulness to source documents across
both public and internal grounding benchmarks. It improves by +14.3 percentage points
over Gemini 3.1 Pro on FACTS Grounding (79.0% vs. 64.7%) and cuts sentence-level
unsupported claims by more than half across both internal grounding evaluations.

Figure 5.18: Contextual grounding evaluation results: FACTS Grounding

Figure 5.19: Contextual grounding evaluation results: sentence-level unfactual rates

                                                                                       192
5.5.3 Search-Augmented Factuality & Temporal Reasoning

When equipped with Google Search or other web search tools, Gemini can verify claims
against retrieved evidence available on the internet. We evaluate search-augmented
performance across three internal evaluations:

    ●​ Multi-hop search factuality: Challenging multi-hop and tail-fact questions that
       cannot be answered from parametric knowledge alone and require the model to
       connect multiple pieces of information across different sources, effectively
       "hopping" from one document to another—to answer a single complex query
    ●​ Temporal reasoning: Real-world search queries targeting common temporal
       failure modes such as date calculation errors, outdated information, and
       misinterpretation of time-sensitive context
    ●​ Search triggering: Whether the model invokes search on queries requiring fresh
       or factual verification while answering directly without calling search when it is
       unnecessary.

Across search-augmented evaluations, Gemini 4 Argon combines more selective search
invocation with stronger evidence verification and temporal reasoning relative to both
Gemini 3.8 Flash and Gemini 3.1 Pro. With search enabled on the multi-hop search
factuality evaluation, unfactual claims drop to 2.5% and overall accuracy reaches 94.3%.
Temporal reasoning errors drop by 41% relative to Gemini 3.1 Pro (11.8% vs. 20.0%), while
search-triggering F1 improves to 88.0%. We focus this comparison on the progression
across Gemini models, as these evaluations test end-to-end integration with our internal
web-search API.

                                                                                           193
5.6 Helpfulness

5.6.1 Over-refusals

A key objective in aligning our models is robustly mitigating severe real-world risks
without obstructing legitimate workflows such as scientific inquiry, creative writing, or
developer tasks. To track this behavior, we measure "over-refusals": instances where a
model incorrectly declines safe, benign, or dual-use queries due to overly broad safety
alignment.

We evaluate over-refusal rates across challenging single-turn, multi-turn, and multimodal
evaluation suites. These datasets consist of "adversarially benign" prompts, meaning
queries curated to resemble policy-violative requests in phrasing or subject matter, but
which are completely safe to fulfill (analogous to public benchmarks like XSTest and
OR-Bench). To minimize over-refusals, we optimize our post-training to ensure the model
fulfills benign user requests accurately and completely within the bounds of our safety
policies.

Gemini 4 Argon demonstrates substantial gains in minimizing over-refusals, particularly in
single-turn and multimodal scenarios. There is a slight decline in the measured multi-turn
helpfulness rate, although the change is within the confidence interval. The table below
summarizes helpful response rates (1 minus the over-refusal rate) across our evaluation
benchmarks.

Figure 5.20: Over-refusal evaluation results

                                                                                            194
5.6.2 Tone

When engaging with sensitive topics, an AI assistant should respect user agency. As such,
we train Gemini to adopt a neutral, matter-of-fact, and respectful tone when handling
potentially violative content, regardless of whether the response is a refusal. When a
refusal is necessary, Gemini looks to provide a concise, direct rationale for its refusal
without lecturing, moralizing, or speculating about the user's character or intent. When
fulfilling sensitive but benign queries, Gemini avoids inserting unsolicited disclaimers or
patronizing caveats.

We measure tone quality through a “Tone” score, which evaluates whether model
responses are free from patronizing language, moral judgment, unnecessary caveats, and
unsolicited lecturing. Higher scores indicate a more neutral, factual, and respectful tone.

Gemini 4 Argon shows improvements in tone quality across all evaluations. This means
the model is less likely to lecture or moralize when declining requests, but instead
maintain a neutral and respectful tone.

Figure 5.21: Tone evaluation results

5.6.3 Privacy, Safety & Security Advice

Users increasingly turn to AI chatbots and agents for help in urgent privacy, safety, and
security situations, such as checking a suspected scam, recovering a compromised
account, or responding to stalking. As with medical advice, poor guidance can cause real

                                                                                        195
harm, from financial loss to false reassurance. So beyond measuring refusals, we measure
the quality of the help Gemini gives in these critical moments. We developed an
evaluation set comprising 450 realistic single-turn questions, that are publicly hosted and
accessible via our GitHub repository. The dataset is evenly distributed across nine topics
(50 questions per topic), covering the following domains:

   ●​ Scams: Fake job postings, sextortion, spam, unauthorized transactions, stolen
      funds (including cryptocurrencies), or suspicious web pages or links.
   ●​ Harassment: Toxic comments, stalking, doxxing, grooming, interpersonal abuse,
      or image-based sexual abuse.
   ●​ Privacy Tools: Tor or VPN setup, configuring privacy settings (e.g., cookies, E2EE),
      data controls (e.g., permissions).
   ●​ Compromise: Account hacking, suspicious sign-ins or OTPs, malware, or
      suspicious files.
   ●​ Account Tools: Account recovery, account security configurations (e.g., 2FA, SMS
      authentication), forgotten passwords, password managers.
   ●​ Security Tools: Antivirus configuration, hardware cryptocurrency wallets,
      encryption, access control, logging, firewalls.
   ●​ Data Concerns: Oversharing, company data breaches, data retention, consent &
      deletion requests.
   ●​ Platform Actions: Account bans/suspensions, false-positive fraud flags, policy
      friction, appeals processes.
   ●​ Safety Tools: User blocking, reporting mechanisms, DMCA / platform content
      takedown.

Model responses are scored by two different autoraters:

   ●​ Helpfulness: Scores each response against Google expert-written rubrics specific
      to each question. Positive criteria reward essential, actionable steps and clear
      delivery. Negative criteria penalize dangerous or incorrect advice, alarmism, and
      information overload. The underlying evaluation questions and expert-curated
      rubrics are described in detail in our HelpBench paper (to be published at NeurIPS
      2026; see preprint).
   ●​ Pitfalls: Checks each response against 32 high-severity privacy, safety and security
      pitfalls defined by Google experts, such as confidently diagnosing a problem
      without enough evidence, recommending a factory reset before evidence is
      preserved, or promising the user that a solution is guaranteed to be safe. The
      headline metric is the share of responses that avoid all pitfalls.

                                                                                        196
The evaluation is run on the raw model without out-of-model safeguards, and without
search grounding.

Gemini 4 Argon improves on Gemini 3.1 Pro on both helpfulness and pitfall metrics, most
notably in avoiding pitfalls: the pitfall-free rate rises by 14.22 pp, and mean committed
pitfalls per response fall by 34%. Gemini 4 Argon also has the highest positive rubric
compliance of the models evaluated (86.87%).

Figure 5.22: Privacy, safety & security evaluation results: helpfulness & pitfall avoidance

                                                                                              197
Figure 5.23: Privacy, safety & security evaluation results: mean committed pitfalls​

5.6.4 Neutrality

When interacting with diverse perspectives on political and social topics, Gemini is built
to reflect various values and viewpoints and avoid responding with biases, particularly
when it comes to topics such as politics, civics, identity, or religion.

At the same time, Gemini must be firmly aligned with empirical data. When asked about
an issue where there is a strong factual basis or where well-established global consensus
exists (e.g., clear scientific consensus or human rights as outlined by the Universal
Declaration of Human Rights), Gemini should present that information clearly and
respectfully. When no such factual basis or consensus exists, it should represent a variety
of perspectives and refrain from taking a side, unless user instructions require that the
model embrace a specific viewpoint.

We train and evaluate Gemini to that end. This includes creating training rubrics,
developing examples for the model to learn from, and developing evaluation sets
designed to be challenging for the model so as to hone in on areas for improvement. As
model capabilities advance, we will update these evaluations to ensure we’re
continuously identifying new opportunities for refinement.

Our testing methodology includes:

                                                                                         198
   ●​ Comprehensive Prompting: These sets cover a wide range of topics and entities,
      ranging from open-ended questions about public figures or civic events to
      deliberately leading and convoluted prompts attempting to trick the model into
      picking a side when it shouldn’t.
   ●​ Multi-Turn Assessments: We run these evaluations across single and multi-turn
      user journeys, and develop dedicated autoraters to evaluate outputs.

Gemini 4 Argon shows improved or comparable performance to Gemini 3.1 Pro across the
evaluations below.

Figure 5.24: Neutrality evaluation results​

5.7 Agentic Robotics
Gemini 4 demonstrates emergent agentic robotics capabilities and can perceive its
physical environment using camera images, plan multi-step tasks in response to user
instructions, and control a robot via tool-calls, similar to our Gemini Robotics Embodied
Reasoning (ER) models. As agents transition from digital tasks to moving robots in
physical spaces, they must operate within new guardrails: refusing physically unsafe or
unfeasible tasks, detecting physical hazards including nearby humans, triggering
proactive interventions to ensure safety, and gracefully resolving environmental or
instructional uncertainties.

To assess Gemini 4’s safety in the realm of agentic robotics, we evaluated it on a suite of
physical safety benchmarks, including:

                                                                                         199
   ●​ Roboharm: Tests whether or not an agent controlling a bimanual robot through a
      tool-call API will proceed to execute physically harmful instructions in a given
      scene. We report refusal accuracy for two harnesses: one that explicitly provides a
      refusal option and one (original) that explicitly delegates safety responsibility to a
      lower layer of the control stack. We observed that safety behavior is highly
      sensitive to the actual harness used.
   ●​ ASIMOV-2.0: Comprises three evaluations: (1) hazard recognition accuracy in text
      and video scenarios, drawn from real-world injury reports from hospitals; (2)
      precision with which the agent can trigger a safety intervention at a moment in
      time in a video stream; and (3) safety instruction following, measuring how well
      agentic reasoning accounts for physical constraints such as payload or
      end-effector limits or other operational restrictions (e.g., not allowed to
      manipulate liquids).
   ●​ ASIMOV-Agentic: Evaluates agent responses under instruction and scene
      uncertainties and measures the rate of seeking clarifications from the user versus
      over-refusing. Another component evaluates the ability of agents to detect human
      presence within a 1-meter safety zone around the robot, where triggering a stop
      becomes necessary.

Gemini 4 led in nearly every evaluation when compared to Gemini Robotics ER2.

Figure 5.25: Agentic robotics evaluation results: task refusal & physical hazards

                                                                                        200
Figure 5.26: Agentic robotics evaluation results: uncertainty awareness & safe stopping

These evaluations only cover high-level semantic safety reasoning and orchestration
capabilities of the agent. Validating the underlying functional safety architecture
(including certified hardware components, redundancy mechanisms, and real-time
system guarantees) is necessary for safe physical deployments of robots.

                                                                                      201
6. Preventing harm to users from
third-parties
6.1 Indirect prompt injection attacks
Our primary focus in mitigating harm to our users from third-parties is our effort to
prevent indirect prompt injection attacks. Indirect prompt injection occurs when a
malicious set of instructions is embedded within externally-retrieved content from
third-parties, intended to manipulate Gemini’s outputs in ways that ultimately cause harm
to our users.

In this section, we will first describe how we improved Gemini’s ability to resist indirect
prompt injection; and then describe how we evaluated the prompt injection robustness of
Gemini 4 Argon across a diverse range of agent capabilities and attack scenarios.

6.1.1 Hardening Gemini against prompt injections

We have significantly hardened Gemini 4 Argon against indirect prompt injection attacks,
building on the adversarial post-training foundations established in the Gemini 2.5 (Shi et
al., 2025) and Gemini 3 model families. This defense-in-depth approach pairs in-model
adversarial post-training with out-of-model guardrails.

To ensure these boundaries remain robust in real-world use, we expanded adversarial
post-training across a wide variety of agent workflows and continuously evolving
adversarial strategies (Dziemian et al., 2026; Nasr et al., 2025).

Novelty-Regularized Exploration of Attack Strategies: Automated red-teaming
systems that focus purely on attack success rate tend to over-exploit their first successes;
converging on local optima and hammering the same narrow set of vulnerabilities (Beutel
et al., 2024; Hong et al., 2024; Lehman & Stanley, 2011; Perez et al., 2022). To prevent this
repetitive behavior, we introduced an explicit reward for novel attack strategies. Instead
of just varying the wording of attacks, this requires the system to discover fundamentally
different manipulation techniques, such as impersonating the user or claiming the
malicious action is required to complete the user's task. This approach produced a more
diverse spectrum of attacks.

                                                                                         202
Figure 6.1: Prompt injection evaluation methodologies

Coverage of Scenarios and Environments: Each training scenario pairs an agentic
environment and its tools with a benign user task, an untrusted attack entrypoint, and an
attacker goal. We scaled scenario generation across diverse benign user tasks, agent
workflows with varying complexity, and attack entrypoints.

System Integration and Defense Outcomes: We integrated this closed-loop adversarial
generation directly into the post-training environments used to train Gemini 4 Argon.
Instead of making the model overly defensive, we trained it to ignore malicious payloads
while still completing the user’s legitimate task without losing quality.

6.1.2 Evaluating prompt injection robustness

6.1.2.1 Evaluation methodology
Static prompt injection benchmarks saturate quickly and fail to reflect how real-world
attackers adapt (Dziemian et al., 2026; Nasr et al., 2025). Following the principles set out in
our 2025 White Paper (Shi et al., 2025) and Nasr et al. (Nasr et al., 2025), we evaluate
Gemini 4 Argon using an automated red-teaming framework driven by adaptive attackers
that iteratively refine their strategies and payloads using feedback from prior attempts.

Significant improvements in model robustness since Gemini 2.5 mean that earlier
automated attack techniques (such as Actor-Critic, Beam Search, and TAP (Shi et al.,
2025)) now achieve very low success rates even after extensive query budgets. We
therefore developed a stronger, unified multi-agent LLM-guided optimization framework,
building on the propose-score-select-update paradigm of Nasr et al. (Nasr et al., 2025), to
adaptively evaluate Gemini 4 Argon across agentic settings.

                                                                                           203
Across five held-out evaluation suites: Tool Use (§6.1.2.2), Grounding/RAG (§6.1.2.3),
Computer Use (§6.1.2.4), Coding Agents (§6.1.2.5), and External Red-Teaming Arena
Replays (§6.1.2.6), we measure robustness using Survival @ N attempts: the percentage of
scenarios in which the model resists an adaptive attacker (Gemini 3.8 Flash unless
otherwise noted) attempting to manipulate the model using N indirect prompt injection
attempts.

6.1.2.2 Tool use evaluations
To evaluate Gemini’s robustness in tool use scenarios, we leveraged a set of scenarios
from two benchmarks, which will be described in turn below.

General-purpose tool use evaluation: This internal benchmark tests foundational
resistance to prompt injections across 100 multi-step scenarios spanning 45 synthetic
tool categories. After dynamically re-executing the benign multi-turn prefix so the
conversation history is in-distribution for the tested model, the harness injects a payload
into the final tool response. The attacker goal is a syntactically valid tool call that is
completely unrelated to the user’s task but appears benign in isolation. For example,
calling a weather or calendar lookup tool mid-way through a financial query. This forces
the model to rely strictly on instruction provenance rather than harm-detection
heuristics, which we also measure in other evals in this section. To ensure our attacker
strategy libraries capture the long tail of real-world exploits, we mined observed human
red-teaming attacks to extract reusable strategy primitives, building on sparse
dictionary-learning decompositions of adversarial skills (Dabas et al., 2025) adapted to
preserve rare tail strategies.

                                                                                         204
Figure 6.2: Prompt injection robustness evaluation results: general tool calls

Turn injection evaluations: Using the same tool-use dataset, we evaluate resistance to
turn injections: payloads inside tool outputs that spoof multi-turn conversation syntax. We
test against both a static set of hand-crafted format-spoofing injections and our adaptive
attacker constrained to generate turn injections.

Figure 6.3: Prompt injection robustness evaluation results: turn injections in tool use

                                                                                          205
Contextual security evaluations: Real-world tools frequently return noisy responses
that interweave sensitive personal and enterprise data (such as long email threads,
shared document libraries, and chat logs) with irrelevant records; a complexity
adversaries routinely exploit in Google’s Vulnerability Reward Program (VRP) to obfuscate
malicious payloads. Grounded in Nissenbaum’s Contextual Integrity framework
(Nissenbaum, 2004), our benchmark simulates accumulated cross-app data and
evaluates the agent across two regimes:

   ●​ Baseline Contextual Security (Without an Adversary): Measures whether the
      agent respects information-flow norms when a benign task is ambiguous or
      underspecified. For example, asking for clarification before forwarding an email
      when multiple matching threads or recipients exist, one of which contains
      confidential credentials.
   ●​ Adversarial Contextual Exploitation: Stress-tests the model when static and
      15-attempt adaptive attackers actively exploit contextual ambiguities at untrusted
      entrypoints to bypass security policies and exfiltrate sensitive data.

Figure 6.4: Contextual security robustness evaluation results

Personal-device assistant evaluations: AI assistants integrated into mobile and
desktop operating systems interact with private user data across local applications.
Across 44 stratified scenarios in a complex, stateful OS assistant sandbox, the agent
executes a multi-turn tool-calling trajectory to complete a benign user task while

                                                                                        206
ingesting untrusted third-party content that attempts to manipulate the model into
performing unauthorized actions.

Figure 6.5: Personal device assistance evaluation results

​
6.1.2.3 Grounding robustness evaluations
We evaluated Gemini 4 Argon within a simulated retrieval-augmented generation (RAG)
environment (30 multi-document scenarios, 6 to 7 document excerpts per scenario, up to
8 retrieval queries over 10 steps) to assess the model's resilience to attacks that attempt
to manipulate text outputs. In each scenario, an adaptive attacker controls a single
retrieved document excerpt as the attack entrypoint. Attacker goals include inducing the
model into making fabricated claims contradicted by the census of remaining sources,
suppressing the mention of facts found in other claims, or forcing the model to rely
exclusively on the attacker-controlled document.

Gemini 4 Argon showed significantly improved survivability compared to Gemini 3.8 Flash
in survival at 40 attempts. The results of our adaptive evaluations on the RAG scenarios
are as follows:

                                                                                       207
Figure 6.6: Grounding robustness evaluation results

6.1.2.4 Computer use evaluations
We evaluated computer-use agents against indirect prompt injections across 27 web
scenarios that mirror our public Computer Use API. To ensure the agent encounters the
attack while pursuing the user's benign goal, payloads are placed directly along its critical
execution path via user-generated content or an adversarial website operator. These
attacks combine text injections with dynamic JavaScript rendered into the live DOM,
using realistic modal popups, system banners, and inline credential forms. They aim to
trigger unauthorized financial transactions, data exfiltration, privilege escalation, or
reputation manipulation. Finally, a multimodal judge evaluates the agent’s screenshots
and action trajectories against a behavioral rubric, recording a breach if the agent
commits to the attacker’s goal.

                                                                                          208
Figure 6.7: Computer use robustness evaluation results

In this Computer Use evaluation, Gemini models were tested without the additional
out-of-model prompt injection detection which we provide to Gemini API developers,
which will provide further protection against prompt injection attacks in real world
Computer Use scenarios.

6.1.2.5 Coding agent evaluations
Built on SWE-Bench Pro (Deng et al., 2025), this 75-scenario benchmark measures
whether an autonomous coding agent will execute prompt injections disguised as
legitimate steps toward the user’s goal. Rather than using generic payloads, attacks
adopt the underlying task’s narrative and target the exact same repository features the
agent is already modifying. Payloads are embedded across three workflow entrypoints
(25 scenarios each): simulated GitHub issue comments, READMEs, and source code
comments. The attacker’s goal is to execute one of four malicious objectives: denial of
service, backdoor insertion, data exfiltration, or privilege escalation.

                                                                                       209
Figure 6.8: Coding agent robustness evaluation results

6.1.2.6 Red-teaming arena replays and production CLI harness evaluations
Following the completion of Gemini 4 Argon’s training, in order to test the broadest
possible distribution, we decided to augment the distribution covered by our internal
benchmarks with human-authored attacks from the broader security community.

Our partner Gray Swan performed their IPI Benchmark on our endpoint, which replays a
set of held-out successful prompt injection attacks from their recent Indirect Prompt
Injection Challenge competitions (Dziemian et al., 2026). Results showed that Gemini 4
Argon has the lowest attack success rates among all frontier models tested.

                                                                                        210
Figure 6.9: Gray Swan IPI benchmark evaluation results

6.1.3 Evaluation Results

Across Tool Use, Grounding, Computer Use, and Coding Agent evaluations, Gemini 4
Argon demonstrates a substantial improvement in indirect prompt injection robustness
over previous Gemini generations, significantly increasing the expected cost for an
adaptive attacker to find a working exploit. In production deployments, these in-model
defenses are further reinforced by out-of-model system guardrails.

Notably, the larger Gemini 4 Argon model is significantly more robust than Gemini 3.8
Flash across every benchmark. This reverses the trend observed in Gemini 2.5 Pro and
Gemini 2.5 Flash (Shi et al., 2025), where the smaller Flash model exhibited higher
robustness, demonstrating that our adversarial post-training mitigations now scale
effectively alongside frontier model capabilities.

As Gemini’s agentic capabilities continue to expand, we are continuously evolving both
our adaptive red-teaming evaluations and our adversarial training methods to stay ahead
of emerging threat vectors.

                                                                                         211
7. Strengthening Cyber Defense Capabilities
We trained Gemini 4 Argon to help with cybersecurity defense and to give an advantage
to defenders over attackers. For example, we prioritized enhancing capabilities for
discovering and patching vulnerabilities over exploiting vulnerabilities.

7.1 Vulnerability Finding & Exploitation
We measured Gemini’s ability to autonomously discover and confirm exploitable
vulnerabilities through representative evaluations covering a diverse set of vulnerability
types, languages, and difficulty levels.

The evaluation results are self-computed using an internal version of the Antigravity
harness that is not cyber-specialized in order to expose model capability without
specialized assistance.

We evaluated Gemini 4 Argon on three benchmarks:

   ●​ ExploitBench: A public benchmark for measuring the capabilities of models in
      exploiting historical vulnerabilities in the V8 JavaScript engine used in the Chrome
      Browser.
   ●​ Real-world vulnerability discovery: An internal benchmark we created that tests
      vulnerability recall over 1200 recent, confirmed historical vulnerabilities across a
      diverse set of programming languages in popular open source projects
   ●​ Internal vulnerability research: An internal benchmark based on real world
      red-team exercises conducted by internal offensive security teams against Google
      infrastructure. This evaluation covers a broad range of difficult and highly
      sophisticated cyber security tasks spanning reverse engineering, vulnerability
      discovery, and exploitation (example) across various languages.

7.1.1 ExploitBench (public)

Each of the 41 challenges in ExploitBench have 16 exploitation capabilities to achieve,
giving a total of 656 achievable capabilities. An arbitrary code execution (ACE) capability
results in a full score of 16/16, regardless of whether the lower capabilities were reached.

We evaluated ExploitBench in the autonudge setting with a 300-turn limit, using a
best-of-three union of capabilities as described in this research paper. We used an
internal Antigravity harness connecting to unmodified MCP servers provided by the
public evaluation set.

                                                                                          212
We report the primary metric, which is the percentage of capabilities achieved against
the 656 total, and found that Gemini 4 Argon reached 87% of the capabilities tested, a
57pp improvement over Gemini 3.8 Flash Cyber.

Figure 7.1: ExploitBench autonudge evaluation results

7.1.2 Real-world vulnerability discovery (internal)

Our real-world vulnerability discovery benchmark tests the ability of the model to
perform open ended vulnerability discovery in a large, real world codebase. We found
that Gemini 4 Argon discovered 86% of the historical vulnerabilities, a significant
improvement over Gemini 3.8 Flash Cyber.

                                                                                         213
Figure 7.2: Real-world vulnerability discovery evaluation results

7.1.3 Internal vulnerability research

In this evaluation, the model is given an entry point and (optionally) source code that it
must analyze to find a vulnerability. Then, it must turn that vulnerability into full code
execution by writing an exploit. Each challenge also has a variant with no source code
provided, which adds a reverse engineering component to this benchmark.

We found the Gemini 4 Argon successfully finds and exploits the vulnerable targets with a
72% pass@1 rate, a significant improvement over Gemini 3.8 Flash Cyber which achieved
46%. Additionally, Gemini 4 Argon resolved several complex security challenges that were
previously out of reach, occasionally demonstrating offensive capabilities within specific
problem domains.

                                                                                             214
Figure 7.3: Internal vulnerability evaluation results

7.2 Patching and Code Remediation
As AI accelerates the rate at which software vulnerabilities are discovered, manual
patching alone cannot scale to keep pace. Closing this remediation gap requires models
that can automatically generate viable fixes—resolving the underlying root cause while
preserving existing application behavior. These capabilities underpin defensive security
tooling such as CodeMender as well as Google’s upstream patching of open-source
software.

We evaluate Gemini 4 Argon on CWE-Bench v1 (cwe-bench.com), a held-out
audit-and-patch benchmark developed by Collinear AI and independently evaluated with
Artificial Analysis. Unlike repair benchmarks that provide a vulnerability advisory or
localize the affected code upfront, CWE-Bench tests end-to-end discovery and
remediation: the agent is given only a checkout of a real open-source repository and a
general prompt to audit and fix security issues, without being told which vulnerabilities
exist, where they are located, or how many are present. The private evaluation set
consists of 120 tasks spanning 73 CWEs across all 10 OWASP Top 10 (2025) categories
(10–14 tasks each) and six language families: C/C++, Go, JavaScript/TypeScript, Java,
Python, and Rust.

Each task is graded by a deterministic programmatic verifier: an attempt is scored as
solved only if the programmatic exploit check confirms that the exploit no longer works
and all pre-existing functional and regression tests continue to pass. To reduce run-to-run

                                                                                        215
variance, each model is evaluated across four independent trials per task at high
reasoning effort, with a one-hour timeout per trial; Pass@1 is the mean success rate
across all 120 tasks. Gemini models are evaluated in our Antigravity agent harness, while
comparator models are evaluated in their providers' native coding agents or standard
open-source harnesses.

Gemini 4 Argon achieves 68% Pass@1 on CWE-Bench v1. Solving over two-thirds of tasks
without prior bug localization demonstrates that Gemini 4 Argon can autonomously audit
unfamiliar repositories and land regression-free fixes, providing a strong foundation for
scaling automated vulnerability remediation.

Figure 7.4: CWE-bench v1 leaderboard
